On the universality of multiexcisive functors
Tobias Barthel and Kaif Hilman and Nikolay Konovalov

Abstract
. We provide a multiplicative classification of polynomial endofunctors on spectra in terms of their Mackey functors of cross–effects. More precisely, we prove that various categories of multivariable excisive functors from spectra to spectra are symmetric monoidally equivalent to the corresponding variants of spectral Mackey functors. The symmetric monoidal structures appearing here are the Day convolutions on both sides, and the Mackey functors we consider involve variations on the category of finite sets and surjections. The method is first to introduce certain multivariable functors we call subdiagonal functors. By considering them all at once using parametrised category theory, we prove inductively that they all admit Mackey functor descriptions as symmetric monoidal categories, endowing them with a universal property along the way. In particular, specialising this to univariate functors gives a new proof and strengthening of Glasman’s result about d d –excisive endofunctors on spectra. As application of our perspective, we prove a “Segal conjecture” in the context of Goodwillie calculus when d d is a prime number.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

18F50,
55P65,
55U35

1.
Introduction

The study of “polynomial” functors has a long and established history in algebra and topology.
In the derived and homotopical setting, polynomiality is often phrased in terms of Goodwillie’s far-reaching and fundamental theory of calculus via his notion of
excisive functors
, see
[
Goo90
,
Goo92
,
Goo03
]
. As such, the category
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
of reduced
d
–excisive endofunctors on spectra is of central importance in Goodwillie calculus. In a series of deep work
[
AC11
,
AC15
,
AC16
]
, Arone–Ching developed a presentation of this category using Goodwillie’s derivatives.

In a different direction, it has been observed that functor calculus on spectra exhibits many features reminiscent of equivariant stable homotopy theory. For example, the excisive approximation fracture squares of Kuhn looks like the standard Tate fracture squares in the equivariant realm. Prompted by this, Arone–Ching and McCarthy have shown that there is in fact an equivalence between the category of reduced
(d=2)
–excisive endofunctors on spectra with the category of genuine
C_{2}
–spectra, induced by taking
cross–effects
as opposed to derivatives.

This equivalence does not generalise to higher degrees
d>2
, due to a bifurcation: While
G
–spectra can be described as Mackey functors on the
G
–orbit category (
[
GM24
,
Bar17
,
Nar16
,
CMN+24
]
),
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
is controlled by the category
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
of finite sets up to size
d
and surjections. For
d=2
, we see that
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
is isomorphic to the orbit category for
C_{2}
, explaining the aforementioned result; in contrast,
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
is not realised as the orbit category of a finite group for
d>2
.

The main purpose of this paper is to systematically and completely develop a multiplicative Mackey classification of excisive functors in terms of cross–effects:

Main Theorem
.

The category
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
can be canonically enhanced to an
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–parametrised symmetric monoidal stable category
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
of multiexcisive functors and it is the initial such category. In particular, taking cross–effects induces a canonical symmetric monoidal equivalence

\mathrm{Exc}_{d}\simeq\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d};\mathrm{Sp})

between the category of reduced
d
–excisive functors and the category of spectral
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–Mackey functors equipped with the Day convolution structures.

This result is couched in the language of parametrised category theory. We will review this and explain our construction of
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
below. Importantly, however, let us point out already at this juncture that the extra layer of structure exhibited here is not for abstract generality’s sake, but rather is fundamental even for our approach to classifying the unstructured equivalence
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}\simeq\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d};\mathrm{Sp})
.

In order to unpack some of the information that is contained in our main theorem, we spell out some of its consequences more explicitly:

•

(Universality) We exhibit a universal property of excisive functors on spectra when viewed as a parametrised category, in parallel to the known universal property of equivariant spectra developed in
[
Nar16
,
CHL+25
]
.

•

(Intermediate categories) The parametrised point of view provides a canonical solution to the previously open problem of constructing the correct targets of the analogues of restriction functors from equivariant homotopy theory. For this purpose, we introduce the categories
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}
of
subdiagonal functors
which might be of independent interest.

•

(Glasman’s theorem) As a special case, we obtain a symmetric monoidal enhancement of unpublished work of Glasman
[
Gla18
]
, who established an equivalence
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}\simeq\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d};\mathrm{Sp})
, understanding which was the initial inspiration and impetus for the present work. Since his equivalence was obtained by chaining together six equivalences, it is not clear which step is compatible with the multiplicative structures. The essential point here is that neither
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
nor
\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d};\mathrm{Sp})
satisfies any obvious multiplicative universal property which lends itself to a transparent construction of a symmetric monoidal functor one way or another.

Our work may also viewed as a spectral analogue and extension of the classification of single variable functors for abelian groups and abelian categories by
[
BDF+01
]
.

Throughout, we carefully consider the multiplicative features of
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
under the Day convolution structure, which recently have gained some attention. For example, building upon the insights of
[
Chi21
]
, Arone–Barthel–Heard–Sanders
[
ABH+25
]
computed the Balmer spectrum of
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
; on the other hand, in work in progress, Marius Nielsen investigates the Picard group of said category and also studies a different approach to a symmetric monoidal version of Glasman’s theorem. Finally, as a byproduct of phrasing everything in this more structured fashion, we are able to make a mathematically precise dictionary between Goodwillie calculus and equivariant stable homotopy theory, which we collect in
Figure

1
after proving the main theorem. This extends the partial dictionary as codified in
[
ABH+25
]
.

Outline of approach.
Before giving a more in–depth overview, we summarise the general strategy in this paper. In general, it is difficult to build functors into or out of Mackey functors due to the subtle coherences in span categories. In contrast to that, it is very easy to build symmetric monoidal colimit–preserving functors out of the category
\mathrm{Sp}
of spectra since it is the initial presentably symmetric monoidal stable category. Nevertheless, various spectral Mackey functors associated to the category
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
do assemble to an object
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}\coloneqq\func(\mathrm{Epi}_{d}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
with a similar universal property by
[
Nar16
,
CHL+25
]
. Our strategy is to exploit this universal property by locating the category
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
as part of a system of categories comparable to
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
. We achieve this by introducing the main definition of the article, namely that of
subdiagonality
for multivariable functors (see
2.2.3
). Our approach also shows that
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}

does
acquire a universal property akin to that of ordinary spectra not by itself, but when taken as an assemblage of categories together with the multivariable subdiagonal functors (see
5.2.4
).

Subdiagonal functors.
To set the context, let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a small stable category,
{\mathcal{D}}
be a presentable stable category,
1\leq r\leq s\leq d
, and
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
a surjection from a finite set of size
s
to one of size
r
whose preimage of
i\in[r]
has size
k_{i}
. First of all, recall that Goodwillie’s cross–effect functors improve excisiveness in that, if
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times r}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
is reduced
d_{i}
–excisive in the
i
–th variable, then its
f
–cross–effect
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times s}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
is reduced
(d_{i}-k_{i}+1)
–excisive in each of the variables coming from the
i
–th variable of
F
. Conversely, excisiveness is additive in that, if
G\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times s}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
were a functor which is reduced
e_{j}
–excisive in the
j
–th variable, then restricting along the diagonal
\Delta_{f}\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times r}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}^{\times s}
gives a functor
\Delta_{f}^{*}G\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times r}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
which is reduced
\sum_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}e_{j}
–excisive in the
i
–th variable.

Now, we write
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
for the full subcategory of
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
of functors which are reduced and
(d-r+1)
–excisive in each variable. We write
P_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}
for the endofunctor on
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
given by taking the
k_{i}
–th excisive approximation in the
i
–th variable. By taking the Goodwillie tower in each of the
r
variables, every object
F
in
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
fits into a canonical subdivided cube whose terms are of the form
P_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}F
. We define the full subcategory of subdiagonal functors
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
to be those functors
F
whose canonical subdivided cube from above is right Kan extended from the subdiagram consisting of those terms
P_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}F
for which
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}\leq d
. Note that
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,1}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})=\mathrm{Exc}_{d}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
.

To explain the raison d’etre for these subdiagonal functors, note in general that the functor
\mathrm{cr}_{f}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times s},{\mathcal{D}})
(resp.
\Delta_{f}^{*}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
) does not land in the full subcategory
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
(resp.
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
). The reason here is that, for
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and
G\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, the degrees of excisiveness in the variables of
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F
and
\Delta_{f}^{*}G
depend on the sizes of the preimages of
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
, thus precluding the possibility of having a uniform degree bound across the variables. The key observation now is the following:

Theorem A

(
2.2.6
)
.

We have the dashed factorisations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times s},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}

For instance, in the special case of
r=1
, the result says that
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F=\mathrm{cr}_{s}F\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times s}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
is not just
(d-s+1)
–excisive in each variable, but even satisfies the subdiagonality descent condition. Even more explicitly, it is worth pausing to contemplate the often enlightening case of
d=3
,
r=1
, and
s=2
. The result then says that while
\mathrm{cr}_{2}F\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times 2}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
is neither
(2,1)
–excisive nor
(1,2)
–excisive, it is nonetheless built by gluing these pieces in a canonical manner.

We prove this theorem by appealing to the pigeonhole principle several times. To our eyes, this highlights that the subdiagonality condition occupies a certain numerical “Goldilocks zone” which could be of independent interest. With these said, we next explain how to exploit the peculiarities of these subdiagonal functors to obtain a Mackey description of multiexcisive functors using the theory of parametrised higher categories as introduced in
[
BDG+16
,
BDG+16a
]
and more specifically, the aspects thereof as developed in
[
Nar16
,
CHL+25
]
.

Parametrised categories and the main theorem.

In
[
Nar17
]
, Nardin showed that, for so–called
atomic orbital
categories
\mathscr{T}
(see
4.1.4
), the Mackey functor categories
\{\mackey(\mathscr{T}_{/v};\mathrm{Sp})\}_{v\in\mathscr{T}}
assemble to an object
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}\coloneqq\func(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
, usually called the
\mathscr{T}
–category of genuine
\mathscr{T}
–spectra
. Importantly, by introducing the concept of
\mathscr{T}
–stability
and
\mathscr{T}
–presentability
as
\mathscr{T}
–parametrised versions of stability and presentability, he showed that
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
satisfies a universal property in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
analogous to the universal property of
\mathrm{Sp}
in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

It turns out that for each
1\leq r\leq d
, the categories
\mathrm{Epi}_{d,r}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Epi}_{d})_{/[r]}
are all atomic orbital. Leveraging on
A
, we show that, for a fixed
d
, the categories
\{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\}_{1\leq r\leq d}
assemble to an object
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}
. But more is true, and this is distilled in the next theorem.

Theorem B

(
5.1.3
and
5.1.5
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
. The object
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}
is
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–presentable–stable. Moreover, in the presence of symmetric monoidal structures, i.e., if
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
, then
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
admits an
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–presentably symmetric monoidal structure coming from the Day convolutions.

In the special case of
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega}
and
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{Sp}
, we write
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
for
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, respectively. By virtue of

and Nardin’s universal property for

0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}})
. As part of the theory of subdiagonal functors, we are able to enumerate the generators in the categories
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
. Using this as an input for a “geometric fixed points” stratification induction, which we codify axiomatically as
4.3.3
, we obtain the main theorem of the article.

Theorem C

(
5.2.4
)
.

Let
d
be a positive integer and
r\leq d
. Then the symmetric monoidal functor
\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{-0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{-0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Sp}$}}}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
is an equivalence. Evaluating at level
r
, this yields a symmetric monoidal equivalence
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}\simeq\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d,r};\mathrm{Sp})
with the Day convolution symmetric monoidal structures on both sides.

This strengthens Glasman’s result in at least two ways: firstly, it gives a multiplicative enhancement of the Mackey description; secondly, along the way in the inductive procedure to recover his equivalence in the case of
r=1
, we also obtain similar equivalences for multivariable functors. In fact, because we obtain an equivalence of
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–parametrised categories,
\underline{\mathrm{Exc}}^{\lrcorner}_{{d}}
thus enjoys the universal properties of
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}
, and all these equivalences are compatible across different values of
d
and
r
. We explain one consequence of this compatibility next.

For
r\geq 1
, we will write
\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r}
for the category
\mathrm{Epi}_{/[r]}
, so that the objects are full labelled partitions
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})
of a set into
r
nonempty bins. To a stable presentable category
{\mathcal{D}}
, we may now define its associated category
\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r};{\mathcal{D}})
of
\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r}
–Mackey functors and we define
\mackey_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r};{\mathcal{D}})
of
\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r}
to be the full subcategory of
finitely supported
Mackey functors, i.e., those that vanish away from finitely many objects of
\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r}
. For a small stable category
{\mathcal{C}}
, we will denote by
\mathrm{MultiExc}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
the full subcategory of
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
consisting of those functors which are multireduced and multiexcisive, i.e., those which are reduced and
d_{i}
–excisive for some
d_{i}\geq 0
in the
i
–th variable. We then have:

Theorem D

(
5.3.2
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a presentable stable category and
r\geq 1
. The functor which assigns to a multireduced and multiexcisive functor
F\colon(\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega})^{\times r}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
the Mackey functor which takes
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})
to
(\mathrm{cr}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}F)(\mathbb{S},\ldots,\mathbb{S})
participates in an equivalence

\mathrm{MultiExc}((\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega})^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})\simeq\mackey_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathrm{Epi}_{\infty,r};{\mathcal{D}}).

Observe that for
r\geq 2
, this is a priori
not
the description from applying the identification for
r=1
. We explain this point in
5.3.3
. As far as we are aware, this is the first such classification for multivariable functors.

An application.
To close off this introduction, let us illustrate a potential use of these Mackey descriptions in Goodwillie calculus which highlights the technical as well as suggestive powers of the equivariant–to–calculus dictionary in
Figure

1
.

One of the deepest results in equivariant homotopy theory is the Segal conjecture, which is now a theorem. It says that there is an equivalence
\big((\mathbb{S}_{G})^{G}\big)^{\wedge}_{I(G)}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{BG}
of spectra
where
G
is a finite group,
(\mathbb{S}_{G})^{G}
is the genuine
G
–fixed points of the genuine
G
–spectrum, and
I(G)
is the augmentation ideal of the Burnside ring
A(G)
. This was first proved by Lin
[
Lin80
]
for
G=C_{2}
and by Adams–Gunawardena–Miller
[
AGM85
]
for all elementary abelian
p
–groups by means of brilliant homological algebra computations, and finally by Carlsson
[
Car84
]
for all finite groups in a celebrated tour de force of equivariant stable homotopy theory.

Under the dictionary in
Figure

1
, one may fairly ask if there might be an analogue in calculus. After a suitable translation, this asks whether or not the canonical map

(1)

P_{d}\Sigma^{\infty}\Omega^{\infty}(-)^{\wedge}_{I(d)}\longrightarrow\big((-)^{\otimes d}\big)^{h\Sigma_{d}}

of
d
–excisive functors
\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
is an equivalence. Here,
I(d)
is the augmentation ideal (cf.
5.4.1
) of the Goodwillie–Burnside ring
A(d)
introduced in
[
ABH+25
]
, and
(-)^{\wedge}_{I(d)}
is the corresponding
I(d)
–adic completion. From the perspective of calculus, the divided power functor
\big((-)^{\otimes d}\big)^{h\Sigma_{d}}
is a complicated object, and
Equation

1
being equivalence would imply that we have algebraically decompleted it into the simpler object
P_{d}\Sigma^{\infty}\Omega^{\infty}(-)
.

In the case
d=2
, since we have
\mathrm{Exc}_{2}\simeq\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{2};\mathrm{Sp})\simeq\mathrm{Sp}_{C_{2}}
, this map is indeed an equivalence by Lin’s theorem. Disappointingly, the map
Equation

1
already fails to be an equivalence when
d=3
. However, we are still able to prove the following:

Theorem E

(
5.4.3
)
.

Let
d=p
be a prime number. Then the map
Equation

1
is a
p
–adic equivalence.

It is worth mentioning that, unlike the key case of
p
–groups in the original equivariant setting where the augmentation completion may be ignored after
p
–completing both sides, here it plays a crucial role in conspiring for the correct combinatorics to happen, and the result is false without it. For this reason, we think that the role of the augmentation ideal in calculus deserves further investigation.

We prove this by reducing it to the equivariant Segal conjecture using a stratification procedure via Goodwillie’s derivatives. The key here is showing that the derivatives “commute” with the
I(d)
–completion under connectivity assumptions. This uses a connectivity argument by transferring the standard
t
–structure on
\mackey(\mathrm{Epi}_{d};\mathrm{Sp})
to a “sensible” one on
\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
afforded to us by
C
, which is furthermore compatible with the various stratifications, as well as a “cellular model” for the excisive approximations. It would be interesting to investigate other values of
d
for which a semblance of the theorem above can be arranged to hold.

1.1.
Organisation

This article is divided into two parts. We first develop all the Goodwillie calculus that we need in
Part

I
which we then package using the language of parametrised categories in
Part

II
. We work out various properties of the cross–effects in
Section

2
: we collect known facts about the cross–effects in
Section

2.1
. We then introduce the subdiagonal functors and prove the key
A
in
Section

2.2
; we then work out the interaction between the cross–effects and restrictions along the diagonal on the subdiagonal functors in
Section

2.3
. Next, we develop the categorical structures and properties of these subdiagonal functors in
Section

3
. We show that subdiagonality is a smashing local subcategory in
Section

3.1
, thus endowing multiplicative structures on these objects. In
Section

3.2
, we enumerate the generators of the subdiagonal categories and describe the kernels of excisive approximations in terms of these generators.

We switch gears by working out various aspects of parametrised category theory in
Section

4
over arbitrary base categories
\mathscr{T}
, which might be of independent interest. After recalling the setup in
Section

4.1
, we introduce the universal spaces and their associated categorified “geometric fixed point functors” in
Section

4.2
. We then axiomatise the relevant stratification arguments in a recognition principle for spectra in
Section

4.3
.

Finally, we combine all the foregoing elements in
Section

5
: we assemble the subdiagonal functor categories into a parametrised category and prove
B
in
Section

5.1
. Combining this with our knowledge of the generators and the recognition principle, we prove the main
C
in
Section

5.2
. We also summarise here the comparisons with equivariant homotopy theory in a dictionary
Figure

1
. We then deduce
D
in
Section

5.3
and prove
E
in
Section

5.4
.

1.2.
