Part I
Calculus

2.
Multivariable calculus

2.1.
Recollections

We recall Goodwillie’s cross–effects as presented in
[
Lur17
, §6.1.3]
and record some of its properties that will be relevant for us. We then record some basic interactions of Day convolutions with the cross–effects and excisive approximations. We do not claim any originality in these recollections and they are recorded merely for the convenience of the reader.

Let
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
be stable categories and
\func_{*}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
denote the category of reduced functors and
\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
the category of
r
-variable functors which are reduced in each variable. For
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
a surjection from a finite set of size
s
to one of size
r
, we denote by
\Delta_{f}\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times r}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}^{\times s}
the associated diagonal functor. By for instance
[
Heu21
, Lem. B.1]
, there is a biadjunction

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times s},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{*}_{f}}

where the biadjoint of
\Delta_{f}^{*}
, denoted as
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
, is called the
cross–effect
.

Suppose we have a pair of composable surjections
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
and
g\colon[r]\twoheadrightarrow[t]
. Since
\Delta_{f}\circ\Delta_{g}\simeq\Delta_{gf}
, by the property of the cross–effect as being an adjoint of restriction along the diagonal, we obtain that the cross–effects are functorial in surjections, i.e.,

(3)

\mathrm{cr}_{gf}\simeq\mathrm{cr}_{g}\mathrm{cr}_{f}.

Next, we recall how excisive approximations and cross–effects interact.

Observation 2.1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a category with finite colimits and a final object, and
{\mathcal{D}}
a pointed differentiable category. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
and
k\coloneqq\sum\underline{k}
. Then, for
F\in\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times n},{\mathcal{D}})
, by applying
[
Lur17
, Rmk. 6.1.3.23]
in each of the
n
variable, we have an equivalence

\mathrm{cr}_{\underline{k}}P_{\underline{k}}F\simeq P_{{\underline{1}}}\mathrm{cr}_{\underline{k}}F\in\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}}).

Warning 2.1.2
.

Be warned that
2.1.1
is a sharp result in that the equivalence
\mathrm{cr}_{k}P_{r}F\simeq P_{\underline{r-k+1}}\mathrm{cr}_{k}F
only works when
r=k
(and of course also in the uninteresting case of
r<k
when both sides are zero for the case of reduced functors
F
). Indeed, let
k=2
,
r=3
, and let
F\colon\mathrm{Sp}\to\mathrm{Sp}
be a homogeneous functor of degree
4
given by
F(X)=X^{\otimes 4}
. Then
P_{3}F\simeq 0
. However,
\mathrm{cr}_{2}F
has a direct summand
(X,Y)\mapsto X^{\otimes 2}\otimes Y^{\otimes 2}
. Therefore,
P_{2,2}\mathrm{cr}_{2}F
is non-trivial.

Next, we record the multiplicativity enjoyed by the cross–effects. Fix a small stable biexact symmetric monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}=({\mathcal{C}},\otimes_{{\mathcal{C}}})
and a presentably symmetric monoidal stable category
{\mathcal{D}}=({\mathcal{D}},\otimes_{{\mathcal{D}}})
, i.e., a presentable stable category such that the tensor product
-\otimes_{{\mathcal{D}}}-
commutes with colimits in each variable. We will often omit the subscript in the tensor products
\otimes_{{\mathcal{C}}},\otimes_{{\mathcal{D}}}
when there is no danger of confusion.

Construction 2.1.3
.

We endow the functor category
\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
with a symmetric monoidal structure

-\otimes-\colon\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\times\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
given by the
Day convolution
,
[
Lur17
, Rmk. 4.8.1.13]
. Informally, we have

(F\otimes G)(X)\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{X_{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{C}}}X_{2}\to X}F(X_{1})\otimes_{{\mathcal{D}}}G(X_{2})

for functors
F,G\in\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Note in particular that when
X=0
, the colimit is indexed simply by the diagram
{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}
. Thus since the inclusion of the zero object is cofinal, the colimit in the formula above simplifies to give
(F\otimes G)(0)\simeq F(0)\otimes G(0)
.

Recollections 2.1.4
.

We remind a few properties of the Day convolution symmetric monoidal product.

(1)

(Functoriality)
Let
f\colon{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
be a symmetric monoidal functor between small symmetric monoidal categories. Then the left Kan extension

f_{!}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}}_{0},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func({\mathcal{C}}_{1},{\mathcal{D}})

refines canonically to a symmetric monoidal functor with respect to the Day convolution products.

(2)

(Universal case)
We have an equivalence of presentably monoidal categories

\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\simeq\func({\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Sp})\otimes{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Pr}^{L})
.

(3)

(co-Yoneda map)
The co-Yoneda map

\text{\char 71\relax}\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\func({\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Sp}),\;\;c\mapsto\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\map_{{\mathcal{C}}}(c,-)

refines canonically to a symmetric monoidal functor. Here,
\map_{{\mathcal{C}}}(-,-)
is the unpointed mapping space. In particular, the unit of the symmetric monoidal structure on
\func({\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Sp})
defined by the Day convolution is the representable functor
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\map_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}},-)
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}
is the unit in the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Recall that the inclusion
\func_{*}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
of reduced functors admits a left adjoint
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
called the reduction functor.

Lemma 2.1.5
.

The reduction functor
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is a smashing localisation on
\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
compatible with the Day convolution.

Proof.

We show first that the kernel of the functor
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is a tensor ideal in
\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, i.e., for any
F,G\in\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
such that
F^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq 0
, we have
(F\otimes G)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq 0
, cf.
[
CDH+23
, Lemma 5.3.4]
.
By
2.1.4
, we can assume that
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{Sp}
is the category of spectra. Then any functor
G\in\func({\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Sp})
is a colimit of representable ones and
F^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq 0
if and only if the natural morphism
F(0)\to F(X)
is an equivalence for any
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
, i.e.,
F
is a constant functor. If
F
is a constant functor, then
F
is equivalent to the tensor product
Y\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(0)
for some
Y\in\mathrm{Sp}
. Therefore, it is enough to show that
\text{\char 71\relax}(0)\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(c)\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(0)
for any
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
. The latter is clear since the co-Yoneda embedding is symmetric monoidal, see
2.1.4
.

We showed that the localisation
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is compatible with the Day convolution. Next, we will show that the transformation (from the lax symmetric monoidality of the inclusion
\func_{*}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
)

\eta\colon F\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}})^{\mathrm{red}}\to F^{\mathrm{red}}

is an equivalence for any
F\in\func({\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Sp})
, i.e.,
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is a smashing localisation. Note that the induced transformation
\eta^{\mathrm{red}}
between reduced functors is always an equivalence because
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is a symmetric monoidal functor. But by
2.1.3
, reduced functors form a tensor ideal, and so in fact
F\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}})^{\mathrm{red}}
is already reduced. Hence,
\eta\simeq\eta^{\mathrm{red}}
is an equivalence.
∎

By currying the variables, this shows that the multi-reduction functor also refines to a smashing localisation.

Corollary 2.1.6
.

The multi-reduction functor

(-)^{\mathrm{red}}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})

is a smashing localisation on
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
compatible with the Day convolution. ∎

Corollary 2.1.7
.

Let
f\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]
be a surjective map of finite sets. Then the (co)cross–effect functor

\mathrm{cr}_{f}\colon\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})

refines canonically to a symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof.

By the definition, the (co)cross–effect
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
is the composite of three functors

\mathrm{cr}_{f}\colon\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})\hookrightarrow\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})\xrightarrow{\Delta_{f!}}\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{red}}}\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}}),

where
\Delta_{f!}
is the left Kan extension along the functor
\Delta_{f}\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\times m}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\times k}
and
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is the multi-reduction functor. By
2.1.4
and
2.1.6
, the functor
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
is canonically a lax symmetric monoidal which preserves the tensor product. However, it does not necessarily preserve the monoidal unit, since the inclusion

\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})\hookrightarrow\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})

does not. Therefore, it suffices to show that the natural map

(4)

\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})}\to\mathrm{cr}_{f}(\mathbbm{1}_{{\func_{\underline{*}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})}})

is an equivalence.

Consider the fibre sequence

F\to\mathbbm{1}_{\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})}\to(\mathbbm{1}_{\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})})^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})}

in the category
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})
. In particular,
F^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq 0
. Note that the map (
4
) is obtained by applying
(\Delta_{f!}-)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\mathrm{cr}_{f}(-)
to the map
\mathbbm{1}_{\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})}
because the functors
\Delta_{f!}
and
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
are symmetric monoidal. Consequently, the fibre of
Equation

4
is
(\Delta_{f!}F)^{\mathrm{red}}
. Since
f\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]
is surjective, the functor

\Delta_{f}^{*}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times m},{\mathcal{D}})

preserves the full subcategory spanned by reduced functors. Therefore, for any reduced functor
G\in\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times},{\mathcal{D}})
, we have

\map((\Delta_{f!}F)^{\mathrm{red}},G)\simeq\map(\Delta_{f!}F,G)\simeq\map(F,\Delta_{f}^{*}G)\simeq\map(F^{\mathrm{red}},G)\simeq 0.

By the Yoneda lemma,
(\Delta_{f!}F)^{\mathrm{red}}
is trivial.
∎

Finally, let us record the interaction of the Day convolution with excisive approximations.

Lemma 2.1.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
. Then the Day convolution
F\otimes G
is
d
-excisive if
F,G\in\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and
G
is
d
-excisive.

Proof.

Consider the endofunctor
T_{d}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
given by

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\mapsto\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}}F(C_{S}(-))

from
[
Lur17
, Cons. 6.1.1.12]
, where
C_{S}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is the
S
-pointed cone, see
[
Lur17
, Cons. 6.1.1.18]
. Since
G
is
d
-excisive, the natural map
G\to T_{d}G
is an equivalence and it suffices to show that

F\otimes G\to T_{d}(F\otimes G)

is an equivalence as well, see
[
Lur17
, Cons. 6.1.1.27]
for the construction of the
d
-excisive approximation.

Since the pointed cone
C_{S}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a left Kan extension, there are natural equivalences

X\otimes C_{S}(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}C_{S}(X\otimes Y)
for all
X,Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Therefore, the functor
C_{S}
is equivalent to the tensor product with
C_{S}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}})
and since
C_{S}
is a finite colimit,
C_{S}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}})
is a dualisable object. Hence, since the Day convolution is constructed as a left Kan extension, the functor
C_{S}^{*}(F\otimes G)
is a left Kan extension along the composite

{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{-\otimes-}{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{C_{S}(\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}})^{\vee}\otimes-}{\mathcal{C}}

which is naturally equivalent to

{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times(C_{S}(\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}})^{\vee}\otimes-)}{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{-\otimes-}{\mathcal{C}}.

This implies that the diagram
S\mapsto(F\otimes G)(C_{S}(-))
is equivalent to the diagram
S\mapsto F\otimes G(C_{S}(-))
. Finally, since the Day convolution commutes with finite products, we have

\displaystyle T_{d}(F\otimes G)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}}(F\otimes G)(C_{S}(-))\simeq\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}}F\otimes G(C_{S}(-))

\displaystyle\simeq F\otimes\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}}G(C_{S}(-))\simeq F\otimes T_{d}(G)\simeq F\otimes G

as was to be shown.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}})
,
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
, and let
\underline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
be a multi-index. We write
\mathrm{Exc}_{\underline{d}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
for the full subcategory of
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
spanned by the functors which are reduced
d_{i}
–excisive in the
i
-th variable.

Corollary 2.1.9
.

The category
\mathrm{Exc}_{\underline{d}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
admits a presentably symmetric monoidal structure rendering a refinement of the left adjoint
P_{\underline{d}}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Exc}_{\underline{d}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
to a smashing localisation.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
r=1
. Then
P_{d}
is a localisation compatible with the Day convolution by
[
CDH+23
, Lem. 5.3.4]
, see also
[
ABH+25
, Prop. 2.38, Thm. 2.39]
. We only have to show that the transformation

\eta\colon F\otimes P_{d}(\mathbbm{1}_{\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})})\to P_{d}(F)

is an equivalence for any
F\in\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
, i.e.,
P_{d}
is a smashing localisation. Note that
P_{d}(\eta)
is always an equivalence because
P_{d}
is a symmetric monoidal functor. Finally, by
2.1.8
, both sides
\eta
are
d
-excisive, so
\eta\simeq P_{d}(\eta)
is an equivalence.
∎

2.2.
Subdiagonal functors

Fix a small stable category
{\mathcal{C}}
, a presentable stable category
{\mathcal{D}}
, and a number
d
throughout.

Notation 2.2.1
.

Let
r\leq d
. We will write
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
for the full subcategory of
\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
consisting of those functors which are reduced and
(d-r+1)
–excisive in each variable.
In the special case of
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega},{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{Sp}
, we will just write
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}
.

The following observation and definition are the key insights which form the starting point for our work.

Notation 2.2.2
.

Let
r\leq d
and
m\coloneqq d-r+1
. We denote by
Q_{d,r}
for the category
(\Delta^{m-1})^{\times r}
which we think of as a subdivided cube. For these cubical objects
Q_{d,r}
, it will be convenient to adopt the convention that
\Delta^{m-1}
be viewed as the linearly ordered category
\{1\rightarrow 2\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow m\}
. Under this convention, we write
Q^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}\subseteq Q_{d,r}
for the full subcategory of those tuples
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})
such that
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}\leq d
, which we think of as truncated subdivided cubes.

Then, by considering the Goodwillie tower of excisive approximations in each variable, we obtain a functor

P_{\underline{\bullet}}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\longrightarrow\func(Q_{d,r},\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}))

where for
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
,
P_{\underline{\bullet}}F
sends
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})\in Q_{d,r}
to
P_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}F
.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
r\leq d
. We write
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
for the full subcategory of the
subdiagonal functors
, i.e., those functors
F
whose associated
Q_{d,r}
–cube
P_{\underline{\bullet}}F
is right Kan extended from its restriction to
Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}\subseteq Q_{d,r}
.

Example 2.2.4
.

We illustrate here some examples of the
Q_{d,r}
–cubes from
2.2.2
and the condition of subdiagonality. In the following diagrams, the subdiagonal functors are those which are right Kan extended from the blue portion.

(1)

For
d=3
,
r=2
, and
m=2
, every object in
\mathrm{Exc}_{3,2}
fits in the
Q_{3,2}
–cube
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{2,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,1}}.}

(2)

For
d=4
,
r=2
, and
m=3
, every object in
\mathrm{Exc}_{4,2}
fits in the
Q_{4,2}
–cube
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{3,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{3,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{3,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{2,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{2,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,1}}.}

(3)

For
d=4
,
r=3
, and
m=2
, every object in
\mathrm{Exc}_{4,3}
fits in the
Q_{4,3}
–cube
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2,2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2,2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2,1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{2,1,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1,2,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,2,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{1,1,1}}.}

Observation 2.2.5
.

Note that
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
are stable subcategories. This is because we are in the stable context, and so
P_{\underline{k}}
preserves finite (co)limits. In fact,
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is also closed under arbitrary limits in
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
as we shall see in
3.1.1
: this is not immediate a priori since subdiagonality is defined in terms of the functors
P_{\underline{k}}
, which do not commute with infinite limits.

We are now ready to state the fundamental insight about subdiagonlity, i.e.,
A
, which affords our approach to obtaining the Mackey description in
C
.

Theorem 2.2.6

(Key pigeonhole principle)
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
. Let
r\leq s\leq d
and
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
be a surjection. We have the dashed factorisations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times s},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}

Remark 2.2.7
.

As pointed out in the introduction, neither dashed lifts are obvious. Without the subdiagonality condition, it is not true that
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
on
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
lands in
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, much less
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,s}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. Similarly in the case of
\Delta_{f}^{*}
. The subdiagonality condition is what guarantees that the images of these functors land in multivariable functors that have a uniform excisiveness bounds across the variables.

As an immediate consequence of
2.2.6
and the biadjunction
Equation

2
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 2.2.8
.

Let
r\leq s\leq d
and
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
be a surjection.
Then the biadjunction in the top row of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times s},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{d,s}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}

restricts to a dashed biadjunction in the bottom row.

The remainder of this subsection will be occupied with the proof of the theorem. To this end, we will need the following two limit cofinality preliminaries.

Construction 2.2.9
.

Let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}
be some positive integers,
k\coloneqq\sum_{i}k_{i}
, and let
\sum\colon\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}\rightarrow\Delta^{k}
be the functor which assigns to
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}
the object
\sum_{i}a_{i}\in\Delta^{k}
(recall also our convention for
\Delta^{k}
from
2.2.2
). Write
q\colon B_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}
for the pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle s\in\Delta^{r}\>|\>s\geq n\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{k}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum}

That is,
B_{n}
is the full subcategory spanned by those tuples
(a_{i})_{i}\in\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}
such that
\sum_{i}a_{i}\geq n
.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a category with finite limits and
B_{n}
be as in
2.2.9
. A functor
F\in\func(\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}},{\mathcal{C}})
is in the image of the right Kan extension
\func(B_{n},{\mathcal{C}})\xhookrightarrow{q_{*}}\func(\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}},{\mathcal{C}})
if and only if for all tuples
\underline{a}\in\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}\backslash B_{n}
, writing
\underline{\delta}_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
for the tuple with
1
in component
i
and zero elsewhere, the
r
–cube

\Big\{F(\underline{a}+\underline{\delta}_{i_{1}}+\cdots+\underline{\delta}_{i_{t}})\Big\}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{t})\in\mathrm{Subsets\>of\>}[r]}

is a limit diagram.

Proof.

Note that since we can factorise the inclusion
q\colon B_{n}\subseteq\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}}
as the sequence of fully faithful functors

B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n-1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow B_{1}\hookrightarrow B_{0}=\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{k_{r}},

it suffices by induction to prove the statement for the inclusion
B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n-1}
. By the pointwise right Kan extension formula, a functor
F\colon B_{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
is right Kan extended along
B_{n}\subseteq B_{n-1}
if and only if for all
\underline{a}\in B_{n-1}\backslash B_{n}
, i.e.,
\sum\underline{a}=n-1
, the canonical map
F(\underline{a})\rightarrow\lim_{(\underline{a}\downarrow B_{n})}F
is an equivalence.
But, since everything in sight is a poset, the comma category
(\underline{a}\downarrow B_{n})
is equivalent to the punctured subdivided cube
(\prod_{i}\Delta^{l_{i}})\backslash\underline{1}
where
l_{i}\coloneqq k_{i}-a_{i}
.

The main point now is that the inclusion
c\colon(\Delta^{1})^{\times r}=\prod_{i}\Delta^{l_{i}}_{\leq 2}\subseteq\prod_{i}\Delta^{l_{i}}
admits a right adjoint since for any number
k
, the inclusion
\Delta^{1}=\{1\rightarrow 2\}\subseteq\Delta^{k}
admits a right adjoint given by sending all numbers in
\Delta^{k}=\{1\rightarrow 2\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow k+1\}
greater than
1
to
2
. Hence, the inclusion of the punctured subcube
(\Delta^{1})^{\times r}\backslash\underline{1}\subseteq\prod_{i}\Delta^{l_{i}}\backslash\underline{1}
is limit cofinal, and so
\lim_{(\underline{a}\downarrow B_{n})}F
may equivalently be computed as the limit of the punctured
r
–subcube. This completes the proof.
∎

The following simple observation will play a basic role throughout the article as it allows us to reduce many proofs about the category
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
to checking on a smaller class of objects which are amenable to pigeonhole arguments.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. If it is
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})
–excisive with
k_{i}\leq d-r+1
for all
i
such that
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}\leq d
, then in fact
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Proof.

By
2.2.10
, it suffices to argue that for every tuple
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
such that
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}>d
, writing
\underline{\delta}_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
for the tuple with
1
in component
i
and zero elsewhere, the
r
–cube

\Big\{P_{\underline{\ell}-\underline{\delta}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\underline{\delta}_{i_{a}}}F\Big\}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{a})\in\mathrm{Subsets\>of\>}[r]}

is a limit diagram. Our goal is to argue that this cube is degenerate in one direction and thus conclude that it is cartesian. For this, we simply observe that by the pigeonhole principle, there exists some
j
such that
k_{j}\leq\ell_{j}-1
, since otherwise
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}\geq\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}>d
contradicting our hypothesis. Therefore, all the maps in the
j
–th direction in the cube
\Big\{P_{\underline{\ell}-\underline{\delta}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\underline{\delta}_{i_{a}}}F\Big\}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{a})\in\mathrm{Subsets\>of\>}[r]}
will be equivalences, and so the cube is degenerate as wanted.
∎

We are now ready to give the proof of the theorem.

Proof of
2.2.6
.

Any surjection may be factorised into a sequence of surjections which is injective everywhere except at two points. Thus, by the functoriality of cross–effects from
Equation

3
, to prove both of the factorisation squares, it suffices to prove it for
s=r+1
and the surjection
f\colon[s]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
which collapses the first two points of
[s]
. For this case, it will be helpful to adopt the notation
m\coloneqq d-r+1
as well as
\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}\coloneqq\mathrm{cr}_{f}
and
\Delta_{2,\underline{1}}\coloneqq\Delta_{f}
to specify to which variables we are applying the cross–effects and diagonal restriction.

We prove each part in turn. For the square (1), let
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. We need to show two things, namely that: (a)
\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F
is
(m-1)
–excisive in each variable; (b)
\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F
is right Kan extended from the truncated subdivided cube in
2.2.2
.

For point (a), using that cross–effects detect excisiveness by
[
Lur17
, Prop. 6.1.4.10]
, we need to show, without loss of generality, that
\mathrm{cr}_{2,m,\underline{1}}F\simeq\mathrm{cr}_{1,1,m,\underline{1}}\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F\simeq 0
where the first equivalence is by
Equation

3
. This will imply that the
m
–excisiveness in the second variable is actually
(m-1)
–excisive. But since
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{cr}_{2,m,\underline{1}}P_{\underline{k}}F\simeq 0

for
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})\leq\underline{m}
satisfying
\sum\underline{k}\leq d
since
F
is a finite limit over these
P_{\underline{k}}F
’s. Without loss of generality, we might as well assume that
F
is
\underline{k}
–excisive. This will involve a case analysis: we know first of all that
k_{i}\leq m
for all
i
. Suppose
k_{2}<m
. Then of course
\mathrm{cr}_{2,m,\underline{1}}F\simeq 0
. Now suppose
k_{2}=m
. Then

k_{1}+k_{3}+\cdots+k_{r}\leq d-m=r-1.

If one of the
k_{i}^{\prime}s
were
0
, then
F
was already the zero functor since it is reduced in each variable. If not, then since we must have
r-1\leq k_{1}+k_{3}+\cdots+k_{r}\leq r-1
, we thus have
k_{1}=1
, in which case we also get
\mathrm{cr}_{2,m,\underline{1}}F\simeq 0
. This completes the proof of (a).

For point (b), by
2.2.10
,
it suffices to show the following claim: for
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r+1})
such that
\sum\underline{k}>d
, and writing
\underline{\delta}_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
for the tuple with
1
in component
i
and zero elsewhere, the
(r+1)
–cube

\Big\{P_{\underline{k}-\underline{\delta}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\underline{\delta}_{i_{a}}}\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F\Big\}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{a})\in\mathrm{Subsets\>of\>}[r+1]}

is a limit. Furthermore, by virtue of
2.2.5
and
2.2.11
,
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is built as a finite limit out of the
\underline{m}
–excisive functors which are
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
–excisive where
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\leq d
. Hence, without loss of generality, we may suppose
F
was
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
–excisive where
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\leq d
. Now to see the claim, it suffices to show that the total fibre of the cube is zero, and for this cube the total fibre is computed as the homogeneous layers in each variable. And so to show it vanishes, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{cr}_{\underline{k}}P_{\underline{k}}\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F\simeq 0.

But using
2.1.1
and
Equation

3
, we see

\mathrm{cr}_{\underline{k}}P_{\underline{k}}\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F\simeq P_{{\underline{1}}}\mathrm{cr}_{\underline{k}}\mathrm{cr}_{2,\underline{1}}F\simeq P_{{\underline{1}}}\mathrm{cr}_{k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3},\ldots,k_{r+1}}F.

Since
F
was
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
–excisive, we see that
\mathrm{cr}_{k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3},\ldots,k_{r+1}}F
is
(\underline{\ell_{1}-(k_{1}+k_{2})+1},\underline{\ell_{2}-k_{3}+1},\ldots,\underline{\ell_{r}-k_{r+1}+1})
–excisive by
[
Lur17
, Prop. 6.1.3.22]
. Since we have both
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\leq d
and
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r+1}>d
, we must have that
\ell_{1}-(k_{1}+k_{2})+1<1
or
\ell_{j}-k_{j+1}+1<1
for some
j\geq 2
, since otherwise we would have
\sum\underline{\ell}\geq\sum\underline{k}>d
contradicting our hypothesis. Therefore, one of the variables of
\mathrm{cr}_{k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3},\ldots,k_{r+1}}F
is
0
–excisive, which implies that
\mathrm{cr}_{k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3},\ldots,k_{r+1}}F\simeq 0
since all functors in sight are reduced in each variable. This proves the claim, and so also point (b).

Now for the square (2), again as in the proof of point (1)(b) above, since
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
is closed under finite limits by
2.2.5
, it suffices to show that
\Delta_{2,\underline{1}}^{*}F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
for
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r+1}^{\lrcorner}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
such that
F
is
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r+1})
–excisive with
\sum\underline{\ell}\leq d
. By
[
Lur17
, Cor. 6.1.3.5]
, we know that
\Delta_{2,\underline{1}}^{*}F
is
(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ldots,\ell_{r+1})
–excisive.

We claim that if
F
were not the zero functor, then
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\leq m
. To see this, suppose
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}>m
. Then
d\geq\sum\underline{\ell}=(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})+(\ell_{3}+\cdots+\ell_{r+1})>m+(\ell_{3}+\cdots+\ell_{r+1})
. Hence, since by definition
m=d-r+1
, we obtain
r-1=d-m>(\ell_{3}+\cdots+\ell_{r+1})
. But then, there are
r-1
terms in the set
\{\ell_{3},\ldots,\ell_{r+1}\}
, hence we must have
\ell_{i}=0
for some
i
by pigeonhole. Since
F
is reduced in each variable, this means that
F\simeq 0
as claimed.

Therefore, by virtue of the claim we know at least that
\Delta_{2,\underline{1}}^{*}F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. On the other hand, since
(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})+\ell_{3}+\cdots+\ell_{r+1}\leq d
, we see that
\Delta_{2,\underline{1}}^{*}F
is even in
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}
by
2.2.11
. This completes the proof of part (2) and the theorem.
∎

2.3.
Beck–Chevalley squares

Our proof of the main theorem via parametrised higher category theory will leverage on an understanding of the interaction between the functors
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
and
\Delta^{*}_{f}
. This interaction, which is of a “double–coset” flavour, will be distilled as
2.3.7
, and the goal of this subsection is to prove this. To this end, we will need some preliminaries in order to be able even to state the result. Let
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
be stable categories throughout.

The following considerations are dictated by the theory of parametrised categorie (see
5.1.2
and
5.1.3
). However, since we defer all explanations of this theory to
Section

4.1
and
Section

5.1
, we shall not dwell on motivating the following construction and shall only mention what is needed to prove the desired result.

Construction 2.3.1
.

Consider a commuting square of finite sets

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This square induces two more commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{U},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{U},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{B},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}

By taking the right adjoints
\Delta_{g}\dashv\Pi_{g}
and
\Delta_{g_{u}}\dashv\Pi_{g_{u}}
of the vertical arrows in the left square above, we obtain via the Beck–Chevalley transformation the following lax squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{U},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{g_{u}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{U},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{B},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{g_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{g}^{*}}

We note that
\Pi^{*}_{g}
and
\Pi^{*}_{g_{u}}
are
left
adjoints to
\Delta^{*}_{g}
and
\Delta^{*}_{g_{u}}
, respectively, i.e.,
\Pi^{*}_{g}\simeq\Delta_{g!}
and
\Pi^{*}_{g_{u}}\simeq\Delta_{g_{u}!}
. Moreover, the natural transformations

\Delta_{f}\circ\Pi_{g}\to\Pi_{g_{u}}\circ\Delta_{f_{u}},

(6)

\eta_{U}\colon\Pi^{*}_{g}\circ\Delta^{*}_{f}\to\Delta^{*}_{f_{u}}\circ\Pi^{*}_{g_{u}}

are equivalences if (
5
) is a pullback diagram, see e.g.
[
HL13
, Prop. 4.3.3]
.

Remark 2.3.2
.

The natural transformations (
6
) are also functorial in
U
. Indeed, let
i\colon V\to U
be a map of finite sets over
A\times_{E}B
. Then the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{*}_{g}\circ\Delta^{*}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\circ\Pi^{*}_{f_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{v}}^{*}\circ\Pi^{*}_{f_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\circ\Delta_{i}^{*}\circ\Pi^{*}_{i}\circ\Pi^{*}_{f_{u}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

commutes, where the right vertical morphism is induced by the unit of the adjoint pair
\Pi^{*}_{i}\dashv\Delta^{*}_{i}
. In particular, if
U=A\times_{E}B
, then the natural transformation
\eta_{V}
is induced by the unit of the adjoint pair
\Pi^{*}_{i}\dashv\Delta^{*}_{i}
.

Notation 2.3.3
.

We write
\mathrm{Epi}
for the category of nonempty finite sets and surjections and
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
for the full subcategory of finite sets of cardinality at most
d
. We also write
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}}
and
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}
for their finite coproduct cocompletion, respectively.

Construction 2.3.4
.

It is an elementary check that
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}}
and
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}
admit pullbacks, cf.
[
Gla18
, Lem. 3.1]
. These may be described as follows: let
f\colon A\twoheadrightarrow E
and
g\colon B\twoheadrightarrow E
be surjections such that
|A|,|B|\leq d
. Then their pullback in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}}
(resp. in
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}_{d}}
) is computed as the left square in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is good}\end{subarray}}}U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup f_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup g_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is good}\end{subarray}}}\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{U},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{B},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\Delta_{g_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}

where a subset
U\subseteq A\times_{E}B
is said to be
good
in
\mathrm{Epi}
if it surjects onto
A
and
B
under the projections (resp.
good
in
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
if additionally,
|U|\leq d
). To distinguish these notions, we will also say
\mathrm{Epi}
–goodness and
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–goodness, respectively. Applying
\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{(-)},{\mathcal{D}})
to this square for different
U
separately and taking their sum results in the commuting square on the right above.

By taking the left adjoints of the vertical arrows in the right square above, we obtain via the Beck–Chevalley transformation the lax square

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is $\mathrm{Epi}$-good}\end{subarray}}}\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{U},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{B},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{g}}

Concretely, this natural transformation is given by

(8)

\eta=\bigoplus\eta_{U}\colon\mathrm{cr}_{g}\Delta^{*}_{f}\simeq(\Pi^{*}_{g}\Delta^{*}_{f}){{}^{\mathrm{red}}}\to\bigoplus_{\text{$U$ is good}}(\Delta^{*}_{f_{u}}\Pi^{*}_{g_{u}}){{}^{\mathrm{red}}}\to\bigoplus_{\text{$U$ is good}}\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}

where the summands
\eta_{U}
are induced by the natural transformations (
6
).

We prove that the lax square
Equation

7
commutes in
2.3.6
, and the strategy is to analyse the left adjoint
\Pi_{f}^{*}\colon\func({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})\to\func({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})
.
First, we show that the projection
\Pi_{f}^{*}\to\mathrm{cr}_{f}
splits as a direct summand and we calculate the fibre.

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Let
g\colon B\twoheadrightarrow E
be a surjective map of finite sets. Then the natural transformation

\Pi^{*}_{g}F\to\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow B\\
g(U)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i}\mathrm{cr}_{i}\Pi^{*}_{g}F=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow B\\
g(U)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i}(\Pi^{*}_{i}\Pi^{*}_{g}F){{}^{\mathrm{red}}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow B\\
g(U)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i}\mathrm{cr}_{g\circ i}F

is an equivalence for any multireduced functor
F\in\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Proof.

We will prove the claim by induction on the difference
|B|-|E|
. If
|B|=|E|
, then
g
is a bijection and there is nothing to prove. If
|B|=|E|+1
, then we can assume that
B=\{0,\ldots,n\}
,
E=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
g(0)=g(1)=0
. Let
(X_{0},\ldots,X_{n})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{B}
. Then,

(\Pi^{*}_{g}F)(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\simeq F(X_{0}\oplus X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

and the projection

(\Pi^{*}_{g}F)(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\to\mathrm{cr}_{g}F(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

splits with the fibre

F(X_{0},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\oplus F(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
, see e.g.
[
Heu21
, Lemma B.1]
. This proves the base case of the induction.

Suppose that
|B|\geq|E|+1
. Then there are
b,b^{\prime}\in B
such that
g(b)=g(b^{\prime})
and we let
q\colon B\to B_{0}=B/(b\sim b^{\prime})
denote the quotient map. Hence the map
g\colon B\twoheadrightarrow E
factors as
f\colon B\xrightarrow{q}B_{0}\xrightarrow{h}E
and
|B_{0}|-|E|<|B|-|E|
. Therefore, by the inductive assumption, we have

\Pi^{*}_{h}F\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j}F.

By applying the left adjoint
\Pi_{q}^{*}
to both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pi^{*}_{g}F\simeq\Pi^{*}_{q}\Pi^{*}_{h}F\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\Pi^{*}_{q}\Delta^{*}_{j}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j}F\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j_{v}}\Pi^{*}_{q_{v}}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j}F

where
j_{v}\colon V\times_{B_{0}}B\hookrightarrow B
and
q_{v}\colon V\times_{B_{0}}B\to V
are the projections. By the construction,
|V\times_{B_{0}}B|-|V|\leq 1
for any subset
V\subset B_{0}
. Hence, by induction, we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{*}_{g}F

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j_{v}}\Pi^{*}_{q_{v}}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j}F

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j_{v}}\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{u}\colon U\hookrightarrow V\times_{B_{0}}B\\
q_{v}(U)=V\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i_{u}}\mathrm{cr}_{q_{v}\circ i_{u}}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j}F\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
h(V)=E\end{subarray}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{u}\colon U\hookrightarrow V\times_{B_{0}}B\\
q_{v}(U)=V\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j_{v}\circ i_{u}}\mathrm{cr}_{h\circ j\circ q_{v}\circ i_{u}}F

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\colon V\hookrightarrow B_{0}\\
i_{u}\colon U\hookrightarrow V\times_{B_{0}}B\\
h(V)=E\\
q_{v}(U)=V\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{j_{v}\circ i_{u}}\mathrm{cr}_{g\circ j_{v}\circ i_{u}}F\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow B\\
g(U)=E\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i}\mathrm{cr}_{g\circ i}F.

Here we obtained the last equivalence because the map between the indexing sets for the direct sums is given by
(j,i_{u})\mapsto j_{v}\circ i_{u}
, which is a bijection.
∎

Next, we will use the splitting of
2.3.5
to show that the lax square (
7
) is actually strict.

Proposition 2.3.6

(Double–coset formula)
.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is $\mathrm{Epi}$-good}\end{subarray}}}U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup f_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup g_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a pullback square in the category
\mathrm{Fin}_{\mathrm{Epi}}
. Then the natural transformation (
8
)

\eta\colon\mathrm{cr}_{g}\Delta^{*}_{f}F\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is $\mathrm{Epi}$-good}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}F

is an equivalence for any multireduced functor
\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}}).

Proof.

Let
W=A\times_{E}B
. Then, by the basechange formula, we have

\mathrm{cr}_{g}\Delta^{*}_{f}\simeq(\Pi^{*}_{g}\Delta_{f}^{*})^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}}\Pi^{*}_{g_{w}})^{\mathrm{red}}.

By the splitting of
2.3.5
, we obtain

(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}}\Pi^{*}_{g_{w}}F)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\left(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}}\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow W\\
g_{w}(U)=A\end{subarray}}\Delta^{*}_{i}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{w}\circ i}F\right)\right)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\colon U\hookrightarrow W\\
g_{w}(U)=A\end{subarray}}(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}\circ i}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{w}\circ i}F)^{\mathrm{red}}.

But then, we have the equivalences

(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}\circ i}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{w}\circ i}F)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\begin{cases}\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}\circ i}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{w}\circ i}F&\text{ if $f_{w}\circ i$ is surjective}\\
0&\text{ if $f_{w}\circ i$ is not surjective}.\end{cases}

where the first equivalence is since
\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}\circ i}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{w}\circ i}F
is already multireduced, and the second one is since we are implementing the multireduction
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
on a functor which is constant in one of the variables.
Hence, the only possible non-trivial summands in the direct sum above are indexed by
\mathrm{Epi}
-good subsets
U
. Therefore,

\mathrm{cr}_{g}\Delta^{*}_{f}F\simeq(\Pi^{*}_{g}\Delta_{f}F)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq(\Delta^{*}_{f_{w}}\Pi^{*}_{g_{w}}F)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is $\mathrm{Epi}$-good}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}F.

By
2.3.2
, this equivalence is given by the natural transformation (
8
).
∎

By
2.2.6
and restricting to
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–good subsets, we may restrict the lax square (
7
) to obtain the lax square

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq{A\times_{E}B}\\
U\text{ is $\mathrm{Epi}_{d}$-good}\end{subarray}}}\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|U|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|B|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|A|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|E|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{g}}

Our goal now is to prove the following:

Proposition 2.3.7
.

The lax square
Equation

9
commutes.

Proof.

Since
Equation

7
commutes, for all
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|A|}}
, the map

\mathrm{cr}_{g}\Delta_{f}^{*}F\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{U\subseteq A\times_{E}B\>\>\mathrm{Epi}\text{-good }}\Delta_{f_{u}}^{*}\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}F

is an equivalence. We claim that for any
U\subseteq A\times_{E}B
which is not
\mathrm{Epi}_{d}
–good, i.e.,
|U|>d
, we have
\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}F\simeq 0
. Given this, we will thus see that the Beck–Chevalley map in
Equation

9
is an equivalence as required.

To see the claim, write
a\coloneqq|A|
. Since
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,a}}
, it is a finite limit of functors
G
which are
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{a})
–excisive such that
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{a}\leq d
. Thus, it suffices to deal with such
G\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,a}}
. We write
K_{i}
for the fibres of
g_{u}\colon U\twoheadrightarrow A
at
i\in A
and
k_{i}\coloneqq|K_{i}|
. By pigeonhole, we must have that
k_{i}>\ell_{i}
for some
i\in A
because we have assumed that
|U|>d
. Thus
\mathrm{cr}_{g_{u}}G\simeq 0
, as claimed.
∎

3.
Categorical properties and multiplicative structures

3.1.
Multiplicative structures

Let
d
be a positive integer,
r\leq d
, and
m\coloneqq d-r+1
. Our goal now is to construct a presentably symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
and enhance the cross–effect functors with symmetric monoidal structures. For this, we will need the following alternative characterisation of the category
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}
. Recall first the notation
Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}
for the truncated subdivided cubes from
2.2.2
.

Proposition 3.1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
. The inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
preserves colimits and admits a left adjoint
P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
given by
\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}P_{\underline{\bullet}}(-)
. Hence,
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is a presentable category and the inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
preserves limits.

Proof.

The inclusion preserves colimits since the excisive approximations
P_{\underline{\bullet}}
are smashing localisations and the subdiagonality limits
\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}
are finite by
2.2.3
. Now, for
A\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, the key observation is that
P_{\underline{\bullet}}P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}A\simeq P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{\bullet}}A\simeq P_{\underline{\bullet}}A
by
2.2.11
, where here
\underline{\bullet}
is given by the tuples appearing in the truncated subdivided cube
Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}
. Let
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
so that
F\rightarrow\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}P_{\underline{\bullet}}F
is an equivalence. Then consider the computation:

\begin{split}\map(A,F)&\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}\map(A,P_{\underline{\bullet}}F)\\
&\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}\map(P_{\underline{\bullet}}A,P_{\underline{\bullet}}F)\\
&\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}\map(P_{\underline{\bullet}}P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}A,P_{\underline{\bullet}}F)\\
&\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}\map(P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}A,P_{\underline{\bullet}}F)\\
&\simeq\map(P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}A,F).\end{split}

whence the statement about the left adjoint. The statements about presentability and limit–preservation are now immediate.
∎

Corollary 3.1.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{ex}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
. The category
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
admits a presentably symmetric monoidal structure rendering a refinement of the left adjoint
P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
to a symmetric monoidal functor which is even a smashing localisation.

Proof.

We only have to show that there is an equivalence
-\otimes P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}(\mathbbm{1})\simeq P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}(-)
of endofunctors on
\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. To this end, simply consider the equivalences

F\otimes P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}(\mathbbm{1})\simeq F\otimes\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}P_{\underline{\bullet}}(\mathbbm{1})\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}F\otimes P_{\underline{\bullet}}(\mathbbm{1})\simeq\lim_{Q_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}}P_{\underline{\bullet}}(F)\simeq P_{d,r}^{\lrcorner}(F)

which are natural in
F\in\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, where the second equivalence uses that
Q^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}
is a finite diagram and that the tensor product commutes with finite limits in both variables, and the third equivalence is since
P_{\underline{\bullet}}
is a smashing localisation by
2.1.9
.
∎

Notation 3.1.3
.

We write
\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}
for the tensor unit of
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
under the symmetric monoidal structure constructed in
3.1.2
. In other words, writing
\mathbbm{1}_{r}
for the tensor unit of
\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}})
, we have
\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}\simeq P^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}P_{\underline{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{r}
where
m=d-r+1
.

Having a presentably symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
in hand, our goal next is to formulate the symmetric monoidal refinement of the cross–effects.

Proposition 3.1.4
.

Let
d
be a positive integer,
k\leq r\leq d
,
f\colon[r]\twoheadrightarrow[k]
a surjective function. Then the symmetric monoidal right vertical map in the commuting diagram from
2.2.6
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}

restricts to a symmetric monoidal structure on the left map

\mathrm{cr}_{f}\colon\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}).

Proof.

Let
m\coloneqq d-r+1
, and
n\coloneqq d-k+1
. Consider the solid commuting diagram obtained from
2.2.8
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,k}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times r},{\mathcal{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{{d,k}}^{\lrcorner}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\underline{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{{d,r}}^{\lrcorner}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\underline{m}}}

Here, all the dashed arrows are smashing localisations, where the left horizontal dashed left adjoints were obtained from
3.1.2
. In particular, the solid horizontal inclusions all obtain an induced lax symmetric monoidal structure which preserve tensor products. By
2.1.7
, the right vertical
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
also has a symmetric monoidal structure.

Our task is to argue that the left vertical
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
has a symmetric monoidal refinement. By the previous paragraph, we already know that the left vertical
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
preserves tensor products, and so it only remains to show that it preserves the tensor unit. That is, we have to show that the natural

(10)

\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}\rightarrow\mathrm{cr}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}

is an equivalence. Consider the fibre sequence

F\to\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})}\to P_{{d,k}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{n}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\ast}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})})\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}

in the category
\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})
. In particular,
P_{{d,k}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{n}}F\simeq 0
. Note that the map (
10
) is obtained by applying
P_{{d,r}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{m}}(\mathrm{cr}_{f}-)
to the map
\mathbbm{1}_{\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\times k},{\mathcal{D}})}\to\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}
because the functors
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
and
P_{{d,k}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{n}}
are symmetric monoidal. Hence, the fibre of
Equation

10
is
P_{{d,r}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{m}}(\mathrm{cr}_{f}F)
. Finally, by taking the left adjoints in the second commutative square in
2.2.6
, we obtain that
P_{{d,r}}^{\lrcorner}P_{\underline{m}}(\mathrm{cr}_{f}F)\simeq 0
.
∎

With symmetric monoidal structures in place, we now prove that the cross–effects satisfy the projection formula. Let
f\colon A\to E
be a map of finite sets. As in
2.3.1
, we have an adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{f}}

such that
\Delta_{f}
is symmetric monoidal and
\Pi_{f}
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{E}
-linear, i.e., the canonical morphism

(11)

\Pi_{f}(c)\otimes d\to\Pi_{f}(c\otimes\Delta_{f}(d))

is an equivalence for all
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{A}
and
d\in{\mathcal{C}}^{E}
. The adjoint pair
\Delta_{f}\dashv\Pi_{f}
induces the adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{f!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f!}}

such that
\Delta_{f!}
is a symmetric monoidal functor compatible with the Day convolution, see
2.1.4
. In particular, since the category
{\mathcal{C}}
is stable,
\Pi_{f}
is also a left adjoint to
\Delta_{f}
and
\Pi_{f!}\simeq\Delta_{f}^{*}
.

Proposition 3.1.5
.

The canonical morphism

(12)

\Pi_{f!}(F\otimes\Delta_{f!}G)\to\Pi_{f!}(F)\otimes G

is an equivalence for all
F\in\func({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})
and
G\in\func({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Proof.

By
2.1.4
, we can assume that
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{Sp}
is the category of spectra. Then the functors
F,G
are colimits of
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}
of representable ones and the desuspensions thereof. Hence, we can assume that
F\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(c)
and
G\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(d)
where
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{A}
and
d\in{\mathcal{C}}^{E}
. Again, by
2.1.4
, we have equivalences

\displaystyle\Pi_{f!}(F\otimes\Delta_{f!}G)

\displaystyle\simeq\Pi_{f!}(\text{\char 71\relax}(c)\otimes\Delta_{f!}\text{\char 71\relax}(d))\simeq\Pi_{f!}(\text{\char 71\relax}(c)\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(\Delta_{f}(d)))

\displaystyle\simeq\Pi_{f!}(\text{\char 71\relax}(c\otimes\Delta_{f}(d)))\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(\Pi_{f}(c\otimes\Delta_{f}(d)))

and

\Pi_{f!}(F)\otimes G\simeq\Pi_{f!}(\text{\char 71\relax}(c))\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(d)\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(\Pi_{f}(c))\otimes\text{\char 71\relax}(d)\simeq\text{\char 71\relax}(\Pi_{f}(c)\otimes d).

Under these equivalences, the canonical morphism (
12
) is induced by the canonical equivalence (
11
).
∎

Recall that the adjoint pair
\Pi_{f!}\dashv\Delta_{f!}
induces the following adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{f!}\simeq\Delta^{*}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}

between the categories of reduced functors. By
2.1.7
,
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
is symmetric monoidal.

Corollary 3.1.6
.

The canonical morphism

\Delta_{f}^{*}(F\otimes\mathrm{cr}_{f}G)\to\Delta^{*}_{f}(F)\otimes G

is an equivalence for all
F\in\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{A},{\mathcal{D}})
and
G\in\func_{\underline{\ast}}({\mathcal{C}}^{E},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathrm{cr}_{f}G\simeq(\Delta_{f!}G)^{\mathrm{red}}
. By
2.1.5
, the functor
(-)^{\mathrm{red}}
is a smashing localisation. Therefore,
F\otimes\mathrm{cr}_{f}G\simeq F\otimes\Delta_{f!}G
. We finish the proof by applying
3.1.5
.
∎

By
2.2.6
and
3.1.4
, the adjoint pair
\Delta_{f}^{*}\dashv\mathrm{cr}_{f}
restricts to the adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|A|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|E|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{*}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}_{f}}

such that
\mathrm{cr}_{f}
is a symmetric monoidal functor with respect to the symmetric monoidal structure of
3.1.2
. The next corollary follows immediately from
3.1.6
and
3.1.2
.

Corollary 3.1.7
.

The canonical morphism

\Delta_{f}^{*}(F\otimes\mathrm{cr}_{f}G)\to\Delta^{*}_{f}(F)\otimes G

is an equivalence for all
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|A|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
and
G\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,|E|}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. ∎

Corollary 3.1.8
.

For every surjection
f\colon[r]\twoheadrightarrow[k]
, the objects
\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
are dualisable, and in fact, self–dual objects.

Proof.

By the projection formula of
3.1.7
and the biadjunction
\Delta^{*}_{f}\dashv\mathrm{cr}_{f}\dashv\Delta^{*}_{f}
, the natural morphism

(13)

[\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r},F]\to\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathrm{cr}_{f}(F)

is an equivalence for any
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. By applying
3.1.7
once again, we obtain that the natural map

[\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r},\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}]\otimes F\to[\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r},F]

is an equivalence for any
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. This implies that
\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}
is dualisable. By (
13
), we obtained that the dual
[\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r},\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}]
is equivalent to
\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathrm{cr}_{f}(\mathbbm{1}_{d,k})\simeq\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}
, i.e., the object
\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,r}
is self–dual.
∎

3.2.
Generators and localisations

Having established the internal logic of subdiagonal functors for a fixed degree
d
, we would like now to relate it to those associated to different degrees. The following result gives the first indication for this possibility, and for it, recall the Bousfield localisation
P^{\lrcorner}_{d,r}
from
3.1.1
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
r\leq b\leq d
. Then the inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}_{b,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
restrict to an inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. Consequently, the Bousfield localisation
P^{\lrcorner}_{b,r}\colon\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{incl}
restricts to a Bousfield localisation
P^{\lrcorner}_{b,r}\colon\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{incl}
.

Proof.

Since the inclusion
\mathrm{Exc}_{b,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is exact and everything in
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
may be built as a finite limit of functors
F
such that
F\simeq P_{\underline{\ell}}F
where
\ell
is an
r
–tuple with
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\leq b
, we only have to show that such
F
are also in
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. But this is clear since
b\leq d
. The final statement is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
factors through
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, as supplied by the first part.
∎

We would like to enumerate the generators for the kernel of the Bousfield localisation above in the case of
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega}
and
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{Sp}
. To this end, we first construct a family of jointly conservative functors from
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
d
be a positive integer and
r\leq d
. Suppose that
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
is such that
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F\simeq 0
for all surjective maps
f\colon[d]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
. Then
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d-1,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
.

Proof.

First, we will show that
F
is
(d-r)
–excisive in the
i
–th variable. Indeed, let
f\colon[d]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
be a surjection such that
|f^{-1}(i)|=d-r+1
and
|f^{-1}(j)|=1
for
j\neq i
. Then
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F
computes the
(d-r+1)
–th cross–effect with respect to the
i
–th variable. By the assumption,
F
is
(d-r+1)
–excisive in the
i
–th variable and
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F\simeq 0
, therefore
F
is also
(d-r)
–excisive.

Now, we will show that
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d-1,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
. By
2.2.10
, it suffices to argue that for every tuple
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
such that
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\geq d
, writing
\underline{\delta}_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
for the tuple with
1
in component
i
and zero elsewhere, the
r
–cube

\Big\{P_{\underline{\ell}-\underline{\delta}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\underline{\delta}_{i_{a}}}F\Big\}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{a})\in\mathrm{Subsets\>of\>}[r]}

is a limit diagram. Since
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, this is true if
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}>d
. Therefore, we can assume
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=d
. Then, we note that the total fibre
D
of this cube is
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
–reduced, i.e.,
D
is
(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})
–homogeneous. Let
f\colon[d]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
be a surjection such that
|f^{-1}(1)|=\ell_{1},\ldots,|f^{-1}(r)|=\ell_{r}
. Since
D
is homogeneous,
D
is trivial if and only if
\mathrm{cr}_{f}D
is trivial. However,
\mathrm{cr}_{f}D\simeq\mathrm{cr}_{f}F\simeq 0
by the assumption.
∎

By induction, this lemma and
2.2.6
imply immediately the following:

Corollary 3.2.3
.

Let
d
be a positive integer and
r\leq d
. Suppose that
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}(\mathrm{Sp}^{\omega},{\mathcal{D}})
is such that
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F(\mathbb{S},\ldots,\mathbb{S})\simeq 0
for all surjective maps
f\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
and all
k\leq d
. Then
F\simeq 0
. ∎

Now we are ready to compute the generators for
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
d
be a positive integer and
r\leq d
. Then the presentable stable category
\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
is generated by the finite set
\{\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\}_{r\leq k,f\in{\mathrm{Epi}}([k],[r])}
of objects.

Proof.

We need to show that this is a jointly conservative set of objects. Firstly, note that by
3.1.4
, for
r\leq k
, we have
\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\simeq\mathrm{cr}_{t_{k}}\mathbbm{1}_{d,1}
for
t_{k}\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[1]
the unique map and
\mathbbm{1}_{d,1}\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,1}}=\mathrm{Exc}_{d}
is the tensor unit. Let
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
, and suppose
\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}}(\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)\simeq 0
for all
r\leq k
and
f\in{\mathrm{Epi}}([k],[r])
. Hence, we see that

\begin{split}0\simeq\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}}(\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)&\simeq\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}}(\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathrm{cr}_{t_{k}}\mathbbm{1}_{d,1},F)\\
&\simeq\map_{\mathrm{Exc}_{d}}(\mathbbm{1}_{d,1},\Delta^{*}_{t_{k}}\mathrm{cr}_{f}F)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}(\mathrm{cr}_{f}F)(\mathbb{S},\ldots,\mathbb{S})\end{split}

where the third equivalence uses the biadjunctions from
2.2.8
. But then for any
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}_{d,r}
, we know by
3.2.3
that the cross–effects evaluated at tuples of spheres are jointly conservative.
∎

We may now compute the kernel of
P^{\lrcorner}_{b,r}
.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
r\leq b\leq d
. The kernel for the localisation
P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\colon\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}
is generated by the finite set of objects
\{\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\}_{b+1\leq k,f\in{\mathrm{Epi}}([k],[r])}
.

Proof.

By the description of the generators from
3.2.4
, we just have to show that for
r\leq k
and
f\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
a surjection,
P^{\lrcorner}_{b,r}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\simeq 0
if and only if
b+1\leq k
. For this, we first make the following computation: let
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}\subseteq\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}
. Then

\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}}(P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)\simeq\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,r}}}(\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)\simeq\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},\mathrm{cr}_{f}F).

Now, suppose
b+1\leq k
, and suppose without loss of generality that
F\simeq P_{\underline{\ell}}F
where
\ell
is an

r
–tuple such that
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}\leq b
. We write
k=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}
where
k_{i}
is the size of the preimage of
i\in[r]
under the surjection
f\colon[k]\twoheadrightarrow[r]
. By pigeonhole, there exists an
i
such that
k_{i}\geq\ell_{i}+1
. Hence,
\mathrm{cr}_{f}F\simeq 0
and so
\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}}(P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)\simeq 0
for all
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}
, whence
P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\simeq 0
.

For the converse, suppose
k\leq b
and as before write
k=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}
. We want to show that
P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k}\not\simeq 0
. For this, just consider for example the functor
F\coloneqq(-)^{\otimes k_{1}}_{h\Sigma_{k_{1}}}\otimes(-)^{\otimes k_{2}}_{h\Sigma_{k_{2}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(-)^{\otimes k_{r}}_{h\Sigma_{k_{r}}}
which is
k_{i}
–homogeneous in the
i
–th variable. This is clearly subdiagonal in
\mathrm{Exc}_{b,r}
since
k\leq b
, and hence
F\in\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}
. In this case, we get
\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{d,k}}}(\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},\mathrm{cr}_{f}F)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbb{S}
, and so
\map_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\lrcorner}_{{b,r}}}(P_{b,r}^{\lrcorner}\Delta^{*}_{f}\mathbbm{1}_{d,k},F)\not\simeq 0
, as required.
∎

Part II
Calculus, parametrised

4.
Parametrised category theory

4.1.
Recollections

We will heavily employ the theory of parametrised higher categories as introduced and developed in
[
BDG+16a
,
Sha23
,
Nar16
,
Hil24a
,
Hil24
]
. We briefly recall here the basic aspects of this theory sufficient for the purpose of reading this article, and we refer the reader to these sources for more details on the theory. The basic objects are the following:

Recollections 4.1.1

(Basics of parametrised categories)
.

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a small category. A
\mathscr{T}
–category
is an object
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}\coloneqq\func(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
, and a
\mathscr{T}
–functor
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
. When the context is clear, we will often say functor to mean a
\mathscr{T}
–functor. A
\mathscr{T}
–space
is an object
\underline{X}
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{T}}\coloneqq\func(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S})
. In particular, since we have a fully faithful inclusion
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}
by viewing a space as an
\infty
–groupoid, we also have the corresponding embedding
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{T}}\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
. For an object
v\in\mathscr{T}
, we will write the evaluation of a
\mathscr{T}
–category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
at
v
as
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}(v)\in\mathrm{Cat}
. Using the automorphism
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{Cat}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Cat}
, we obtain the parametrised version of the opposite category functor
(-)^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\mathrm{op}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\textstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{-0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.8pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.73909pt\raisebox{0.30612pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}}}}}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
.

By an
object
in a
\mathscr{T}
–category
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, we will mean a
\mathscr{T}
–functor
x\colon\underline{\ast}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
. We have a
global sections functor
given by
\Gamma_{\mathscr{T}}\coloneqq\lim_{\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}}}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}=\func(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
.

For
v\in\mathscr{T}
, writing (by a mild abuse of notation)
v\colon\mathscr{T}_{/v}\rightarrow\mathscr{T}
for the forgetful functor, we may obtain a
\mathscr{T}_{/v}
– from a
\mathscr{T}
–category by considering its image under the basechange functor
v^{*}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}=\func(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})\rightarrow\func(\mathscr{T}_{/v}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})=\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}_{/v}}
.

There are natural refinements of
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{T}}
to become themselves
\mathscr{T}
–categories (in a larger universe), which we write as
\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{S}}
respectively. Concretely, for example, the value of
\underline{\mathrm{Cat}}
at
v\in\mathscr{T}
is given by
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}_{/v}}\in\mathrm{Cat}
. There is a natural
mapping
\mathscr{T}
–space functor
associated to any
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
denoted as

\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip .

The category
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
is cartesian closed. In particular, for any two
\mathscr{T}
–categories
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
, we may obtain the
\mathscr{T}
–category of
\mathscr{T}
–functors

\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
given by taking the internal hom object. Applying global sections to
\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
yields the
category
\func_{\mathscr{T}}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})\coloneqq\Gamma_{\mathscr{T}}\underline{\func}(\underline{{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{{\mathcal{D}}})
of
\mathscr{T}
–functors from
\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
to
\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
.

There is a good theory of parametrised adjunctions, and one way to obtain this is just to view
\mathrm{Cat}_{\mathscr{T}}
with its natural
(\infty,2)
–categorical refinement. For example, a
\mathscr{T}
–adjunction
L\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightleftharpoons\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}:R

is the datum of functors
L\colon\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}
and
R\colon\underline{{\mathcal{D}}}\rightarrow\underline{{\mathcal{C}}}
together with an equivalence
\mathchoice{\mbox{\uline{\phantom{$\displaystyle\map$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\displaystyle\map$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.8pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.30612pt\raisebox{0.73909pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.56567pt\raisebox{0.56567pt}[0.0pt]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip
